### S1Q02F · followup · 30 words · clip 10.6 s · first detection +1.6 s from the end of the clip · coverage 90 %

> What can you conclude from a PR AUC of 0.38 and an ROC AUC of 0.88, and what else would you need before deciding the model is useful?

**In-app answer 1** — whisper, +1.6 s → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 1)
_grader: Correctly frames 0.38 PR-AUC against the 9.5 percent base rate and names cost-based thresholding; 93 words._

heard: "What can you conclude from a PRAUC of 0.38 and an ROC AUC of 0.88? And what else"

> A PR-AUC of 0.38 is actually quite strong given our base churn rate of roughly 9.5%, meaning the model is about four times better than random guessing at identifying churners. While the 0.88 ROC-AUC shows good overall separation, in a churn context, I prioritize the PR-AUC because it focuses on the minority class. To decide if it's truly useful, I’d look at the business cost of false positives versus false negatives—specifically, the cost of a retention offer versus the lost lifetime value of a churned subscriber—to set the optimal decision threshold.

**Arm gemini-3.1-flash-lite_captured-low-r2** — TTFT 6.5 s, 117 words → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 1)

> I would start with a left join between the snapshots table and the events table to ensure we preserve records for customers with zero calls. I'd select the customer ID and snapshot time, then perform a count on the event ID. In the join condition, I’d filter for the support call event type, and define the time window as between thirty days before the snapshot and the snapshot time itself. To prevent leakage, I’d add an explicit clause that the ingested at time must be less than or equal to the snapshot time. Finally, I’d group by the customer ID and snapshot time to aggregate the results.

**Arm gemini-3.1-flash-lite_captured-low-r2** — TTFT 3.5 s, 134 words → **weak** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 0)
_grader: SQL is correct on left join, window and ingestion cutoff, but it is a raw code block._

> I'd approach this with a LEFT JOIN to ensure we keep customers with zero calls, using a correlated subquery or a lateral join to handle the time window constraints.
> 
> sql
> SELECT 
>     s.customer_id, 
>     s.snapshot_time, 
>     COUNT(e.customer_id) AS support_call_count
> FROM snapshots s
> LEFT JOIN events e ON s.customer_id = e.customer_id
>     AND e.event_type = 'support_call'
>     AND e.event_time >= s.snapshot_time - INTERVAL '30 days'
>     AND e.event_time < s.snapshot_time
>     AND e.ingested_at_time <= s.snapshot_time
> GROUP BY s.customer_id, s.snapshot_time;
> 
>

### S1Q08F · followup · 12 words · clip 5.8 s · first detection +0.5 s from the end of the clip · coverage 100 %

> How could repeatedly training on customers affected by previous campaigns introduce bias?

**In-app answer 1** — whisper, +1.4 s → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 1)
_grader: Names the feedback loop and the counterfactual fix correctly; 106 words is long for speech._

heard: "How could repeatedly training on customers affected by previous campaigns introd"

> That creates a classic selection bias where the model conflates the intervention with the customer's underlying propensity to churn. If I train on data where a retention campaign was active, the model learns that the discount caused the retention, rather than identifying the customers who were actually at risk. 
> 
> This creates a feedback loop where the model starts targeting the wrong segments—either over-targeting people who don't need the incentive or missing those who would have churned anyway. To fix this, I treat the campaign as a separate feature or use propensity score matching to isolate the counterfactual—essentially asking what would have happened if we hadn't intervened.

**Arm gemini-3.1-flash-lite_captured-low-r2** — TTFT 6.2 s, 101 words → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 1)
_grader: Feedback-loop mechanism is exactly right; about 101 words with markdown emphasis marks._

> It creates a classic feedback loop where the model starts learning the *campaign's* effect rather than the user's *true* churn risk. If we train on historical data where we targeted high-risk users, the model sees that those users didn't churn—thanks to the intervention—and it incorrectly associates those behaviors with low risk. 
> 
> Essentially, the model stops predicting churn and starts predicting who is *responsive to marketing*. To fix this, I move toward uplift modeling or causal inference, where we isolate the incremental impact of the campaign to distinguish between people who will stay regardless, and the "persuadables" who actually need the intervention.

**Arm gemini-3.1-flash-lite_captured-low-r3** — TTFT 2.9 s, 78 words → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 1)
_grader: Correct feedback-loop and distribution-shift explanation, but stray markdown asterisks around a word need editing out._

> It creates a feedback loop where the model learns to predict the intervention rather than the underlying customer behavior. If we only train on customers who received a retention offer, the model might incorrectly associate the offer itself with lower churn, rather than the customer's actual propensity to leave. Over time, this shifts the distribution of your training data away from the true population, making the model increasingly blind to customers who would have churned *without* an intervention.

> It creates a dangerous feedback loop where the model confuses the campaign's success with the customer's natural behavior. If we only train on outcomes where a retention offer was applied, the model learns that the offer prevents churn, rather than identifying the customer's inherent risk. This introduces selection bias, because we’re essentially training on a non-representative sample of users who were treated by our own interventions. To mitigate this, I’d either include the treatment as a feature or, ideally, switch to uplift modeling to isolate the incremental impact of the campaign from the customer's baseline churn probability.

This allows the model to learn the difference between the campaign's effect and the customer's actual likelihood to churn, preventing the model from just reinforcing its own past decisions.

### S1Q10F · followup · 23 words · clip 10.0 s · first detection +1.8 s from the end of the clip · coverage 100 %

> The application receives an authorization error after deployment. How would you distinguish an identity problem from a permissions problem or a network problem?

**In-app answer 1** — whisper, +1.8 s → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 1)

heard: "The application receives an authorization error after deployment. How would you "

> I’d start by checking the logs for the specific error code. An identity issue usually shows up as a failure to acquire a token, so I’d inspect the Managed Identity endpoint availability or check if the service principal is actually attached to the pod or VM.
> 
> If the token is acquired but the request is rejected, it’s a permissions problem. I’d verify the RBAC assignments in Azure—specifically checking if the service’s object ID has the correct role on the target resource, like the PostgreSQL server or Blob Storage.
> 
> If the request times out or gives a connection reset, that’s a network issue. I’d use tcpping or curl from within the service container to test connectivity to the target endpoint’s private IP, which confirms whether the Network Security Group or Private Link is blocking the traffic.

### S2Q04F · followup · 24 words · clip 8.0 s · first detection −1.7 s from the end of the clip · coverage 100 %

_grader: Right on incomparable score scales and weighted reciprocal rank fusion; 119 words._

heard: "Why might you use Rank Fusion instead of adding the raw scores, and how would yo"

> Raw scores from different retrieval methods, like BM25 and vector cosine similarity, exist on completely different scales and distributions, making them impossible to compare directly. Adding them together is mathematically invalid because a high score in one system doesn't mean the same thing as a high score in another. RRF solves this by normalizing based on rank position, which is a universal metric across any retrieval source.
> 
> To add configurable weights, I would implement a weighted reciprocal rank fusion where I multiply the reciprocal rank by a specific coefficient for each source before summing. This allows me to prioritize a high-precision source, like a vector database for semantic matches, over a keyword-based source if the use case demands it.

RRF is superior because it relies on the relative rank position, which is consistent regardless of the underlying scoring model. heard: "Construct an example where that greedy policy gives poor coverage, and tell me h"

> A greedy policy fails when the top results are semantically redundant—like five paragraphs all confirming the same revenue figure while missing the context on expenses needed for a full answer. To fix this, I use Maximum Marginal Relevance, which penalizes new chunks that are too similar to what's already selected, forcing diversity. For multi-hop dependencies, I use agentic decomposition. The LLM breaks the complex question into sub-queries, executes separate retrievals for each, and synthesizes the result. If the agent detects a missing link, it triggers a secondary retrieval loop to fetch that specific missing information.

_grader: Max delivery count to dead-letter and tombstone versioning for the delete race; 108 words._

heard: "How would you handle poison documents? Dead letter replay, and a delete event ra"

> I handle poison documents by configuring a max delivery count on the Service Bus, which automatically pushes failures to the Dead Letter Queue. I don't just blindly replay; I’d trigger an alert to Application Insights to inspect the error, patch the ingestion logic, and then re-queue the message. 
> 
> For the race condition, I use optimistic concurrency control in Cosmos DB. I include a version field in every document record. When a delete event arrives, it writes a tombstone with a higher version number. If a late-arriving update tries to write, the database rejects it because the version is older than the tombstone, ensuring the delete state is preserved.
